<a id='question-150'></a>

### Question 150: Psychopharmacology: Part 3 Focus 50 (Elevated Blood Pressure)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the differential diagnosis and management of elevated blood pressure in the context of psychopharmacology, specifically differentiating medication side effects from underlying medical conditions, and applying ethical principles regarding patient autonomy and informed consent when managing potential adverse effects. (理解精神药理学背景下血压升高的鉴别诊断和管理，区分药物副作用和潜在的潜在医学状况，并应用有关患者自主权和知情同意的伦理原则来管理潜在的不良反应。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of major depressive disorder (MDD) presents to his primary care physician complaining of headaches and dizziness over the past week. He has been stable on sertraline 100mg daily for the past 6 months, which effectively treated his depression. His blood pressure, measured today, is 165/98 mmHg. He denies any changes in diet, exercise, or alcohol consumption. He reports taking his sertraline as prescribed. His past medical history includes hypertension, which was previously well-controlled with lisinopril 20mg daily. He also has hyperlipidemia, managed with atorvastatin 40mg daily. He denies chest pain, shortness of breath, or visual disturbances. His physical exam reveals no other significant findings except for the elevated blood pressure. His physician suspects the elevated blood pressure might be related to his sertraline treatment, although sertraline is not typically associated with significant hypertension. The physician discusses the potential link with the patient, explaining that while uncommon, sertraline can sometimes elevate blood pressure, and other causes need to be ruled out. The patient expresses concern about stopping his sertraline, as he fears his depression will return. He asks the physician to consider alternative explanations before making any changes to his medication. The physician orders a comprehensive metabolic panel, lipid panel, urinalysis, and an electrocardiogram (ECG). The results are unremarkable except for the elevated blood pressure. The physician also checks the patient's medication list and confirms he is only taking sertraline, lisinopril, and atorvastatin. The physician considers the possibility of sertraline-induced hypertension, drug interaction, non-adherence to lisinopril, or worsening of his underlying hypertension. Given the patient's concern about stopping sertraline and the potential for depression relapse, what is the MOST appropriate next step?

(A) Immediately discontinue sertraline due to the significant elevation in blood pressure, prioritizing cardiovascular safety over mental health stability.
(B) Initiate a second antihypertensive medication in addition to lisinopril to manage the elevated blood pressure while continuing sertraline, as the benefits of treating depression likely outweigh the risks of mild hypertension.
(C) Counsel the patient on lifestyle modifications (diet, exercise) and closely monitor his blood pressure for another week before making any medication changes, as the elevation might be transient or multifactorial.
(D) Perform a thorough medication reconciliation, including over-the-counter medications and supplements, and rule out potential drug interactions or non-adherence to lisinopril before attributing the hypertension to sertraline.
(E) Switch the patient to an alternative antidepressant with a lower risk of causing hypertension, such as bupropion, while closely monitoring for signs of depression relapse.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Behavioral/Statistical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with MDD, hypertension, hyperlipidemia.
*   **Medications:** Sertraline (SSRI) 100mg daily (stable for 6 months), Lisinopril (ACE inhibitor) 20mg daily, Atorvastatin.
*   **Presenting Complaint:** Headaches, dizziness, BP 165/98 mmHg (elevated).
*   **History:** Stable on sertraline, previously well-controlled hypertension. Denies lifestyle changes.
*   **Differential Diagnosis:** Sertraline-induced hypertension (uncommon), worsening underlying hypertension, drug interaction, non-adherence to lisinopril, other medical causes (ruled out by initial labs).
*   **Patient Concern:** Fear of depression relapse if sertraline is stopped.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST appropriate *next* step, considering the differential diagnosis and patient factors.

##### 2. Conceptual, Neurobiological & Ethical Deep-Dive (行为医学、脑机制、统计原理与医学伦理深度剖析)
*   **Sertraline and BP:** While SSRIs are generally not strongly associated with hypertension, case reports exist. The elevation is significant (165/98 mmHg) but the patient was previously controlled on lisinopril.
*   **Differential Diagnosis:** The vignette highlights several possibilities. Worsening underlying hypertension is common in this age group. Non-adherence to lisinopril is a frequent cause of uncontrolled hypertension. Drug interactions are possible, although less likely with this specific combination. Sertraline is a less likely but possible cause.
*   **Ethical Considerations:** Patient autonomy is key. The patient is concerned about stopping sertraline due to fear of relapse. The physician must respect this concern and provide sufficient information to make an informed decision.
*   **Next Step Logic:** Before attributing the hypertension to sertraline (which is uncommon) or making drastic changes like stopping the antidepressant (Option A) or switching medications (Option E), it's crucial to rule out more common and easily addressable causes. Adding another antihypertensive (Option B) might be necessary eventually, but it doesn't address the underlying cause and adds another medication. Lifestyle modifications (Option C) are always important but unlikely to resolve a significant acute elevation without addressing potential medication issues first.
*   **Medication Reconciliation:** This involves a detailed review of *all* medications, including over-the-counter drugs, supplements, and adherence. This is a standard step in evaluating unexplained changes in vital signs or clinical status, especially in patients on multiple medications. Ruling out non-adherence to lisinopril or interactions is paramount before considering sertraline as the culprit.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** Immediately discontinuing sertraline is too drastic. Sertraline is not a common cause of significant hypertension, and stopping it abruptly risks depression relapse, which the patient fears. A thorough evaluation is needed first. This option prioritizes a potential, uncommon side effect over the patient's established mental health stability and fails to rule out more likely causes.
- (B): **Incorrect.** Adding another antihypertensive addresses the symptom (high BP) but not the potential underlying cause. It also increases the patient's medication burden without investigating why his previously controlled hypertension has worsened. While potentially necessary later, it's not the *most appropriate next step* in the diagnostic process.
- (C): **Incorrect.** While lifestyle modifications are important for hypertension management, they are unlikely to be sufficient for an acute, significant elevation (165/98 mmHg) in a patient previously controlled on medication. Furthermore, this option delays addressing potential medication-related issues (non-adherence, interaction, sertraline side effect). It's not the most immediate or appropriate *next* step in this clinical scenario.
- (E): **Incorrect.** Switching antidepressants is a significant intervention with potential risks (discontinuation syndrome, lack of efficacy, side effects of the new drug). It should only be considered after a thorough evaluation has ruled out other causes for the hypertension and if sertraline is strongly suspected as the cause. This option jumps to a conclusion without sufficient evidence.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆概念横向对比):
| Feature             | Correct Answer (D)                                  | Incorrect Option (A)                                     | Incorrect Option (B)                                     | Incorrect Option (C)                                     | Incorrect Option (E)                                     |
| :------------------ | :------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Focus**   | Diagnostic Evaluation (Rule out common causes)      | Immediate Treatment (Stop potential offending agent)     | Symptomatic Treatment (Add medication)                  | Lifestyle Intervention (Delay definitive action)         | Treatment Change (Switch medication)                    |
| **Underlying Cause** | Unclear; needs investigation                       | Assumes sertraline is the cause                          | Ignores underlying cause                                 | Ignores potential medication issues                      | Assumes sertraline is the cause                          |
| **Patient Factors**  | Considers patient's fear of depression relapse     | Ignores patient's fear of relapse                        | Ignores patient's fear of relapse                        | Ignores patient's fear of relapse                        | Ignores patient's fear of relapse                        |
| **Appropriateness**  | Most appropriate *next* step in evaluation         | Premature and potentially harmful                         | Premature and doesn't address root cause                 | Insufficient for acute elevation                          | Premature and potentially harmful                         |
| **Key Concept**     | Medication Reconciliation, Differential Diagnosis | Prioritizing potential rare side effect over common causes | Symptomatic management without diagnosis                 | Lifestyle changes as primary intervention                | Switching medication without full evaluation             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Always perform a thorough medication reconciliation and rule out common causes (like non-adherence) before attributing a new symptom or sign to a less common medication side effect, especially when the patient is stable on the medication and has concerns about changing treatment. Consider the patient's overall clinical picture, including psychiatric stability and ethical considerations like autonomy.
Key Differentials: Worsening underlying hypertension, non-adherence to antihypertensive medication, drug interactions, less commonly, antidepressant-induced hypertension.
